Towards the type safety of Pure Subtype Systems (Full version)
Valentin Pasquale, Álvaro García-Pérez

Abstract
Hutchins’ Pure Subtype Systems (PSS) offer a unified framework for types and terms, promising significant advancements in language design for features like dependent types and higher-order subtyping. However, the theory has been hampered by a critical gap: a proof of type safety has remained an open problem for over a decade. The original attempt to prove this property relied on the conjectured commutativity of two fundamental reduction relations, equivalence and subtyping. Proving transitivity elimination, however, requires this commutativity, a property that is notoriously difficult to establish for higher-order subtyping systems. In this paper, we address this issue by introducing Machine-Based PSS (MPSS), a novel reformulation of the original system. MPSS integrates a continuation stack mechanism, reminiscent of the Krivine Abstract Machine, to keep track of arguments that are passed during function application, enabling more fine-grained reductions. This architectural change exposes crucial intermediate reduction steps that were absent in the original PSS. The primary contribution of our work is a direct proof that the equivalence and subtyping reductions in MPSS commute. This result formally establishes transitivity elimination, which is the cornerstone of the inversion lemma required for type safety. We conclude by outlining a pathway from our foundational result to a complete, type-safe system, thereby paving the way for the practical realization of PSS-based languages.

1
Introduction

Hutchins’ Pure Subtype Systems (PSS for short)
[
19
,
18
]
have been
proposed as a novel approach to type theory that enables a number of advanced language
features for extensibility and genericity. Among other benefits, PSS (hereafter in the
singular) harmoniously solves both the expression problem
[
30
,
32
]
and the
tag-elimination problem
[
18
]
. PSS’s expressivity allows one to implement
extensible modules based on deep mixin composition, which is a key result for a type
system
[
18
]
. By blurring the distinction between types and terms, PSS
naturally combines dependent types and higher-order subtyping, which makes the approach
very promising as a basis for generic, modular, and extensible programming. However, a
proof of type safety for PSS is conspicuously lacking. This crucial property has only
been shown to hold under the conjecture that two key reduction relations in the system,
equivalence and subtyping, commute
[
18
]
. The commutativity of these two
reductions is an instance of a recurrent problem in higher-order subtyping known as

transitivity elimination
. Consequently, the type safety of PSS has remained an
open problem for more than a decade.

In this paper, we resolve this long-standing open problem by introducing a novel
reformulation of Hutchins’ PSS. Our system is based on a mechanism reminiscent of the
Krivine Abstract Machine, it allows more fine-grained reductions and exposes crucial
intermediate reduction steps that were absent in the original PSS. This architectural
change allows for a direct and elegant proof of transitivity elimination.

Contrary to traditional type systems, PSS harmoniously mixes terms and types. Consider the
natural number
3
and the type
\mathit{Nat}
of natural numbers. In PSS one can have a term that
encodes
\mathit{Nat}+3
with the naive Church encoding of naturals, which type-checks to

\mathit{Nat}
. Terms can also be used instead of types: for instance the function

\lambda x\,{\leq}\,3.x
, whose subtype annotation is
3
(we write
\,{\leq}\,
for the
subtyping relation, that is, the substitute of typing in our calculus), is a valid
expression. The latter example is an instance of
bounded quantification

[
25
]
where the term
3
is the singleton type
\{3\}
and the subtyping
relation is akin to the subset relation.

By replacing the typing relation with a more general subtyping relation, PSS greatly
increases expressivity. Since terms and types belong to the same syntactic kind, PSS
naturally subsumes dependent types and higher-order subtyping. This unification enables a
host of advanced language features for extensibility, genericity, and efficiency,
including virtual types, recursive types, deep mixin composition, feature-oriented
programming, bounded quantification, and partial evaluation

[
19
,
32
,
18
]
.

In the original work on PSS, Hutchins introduces a
declarative
system with two
relations: one for equivalence (akin to
\beta
-equivalence) and another for subtyping,
both of which are defined with transitive closure. The proof of type safety requires a

type preservation
property, which states that the type of a program is preserved
under evaluation. This property, in turn, hinges on a critical inversion lemma: any two
functions in the subtyping relation must have equivalent subtype annotations. This lemma
cannot be proven directly in the declarative system due to the presence of transitivity,
which introduces intermediate terms that may not be structurally related to the functions
being compared. Proving type preservation thus amounts to showing that transitivity is an
admissible rule in the system.

In order to prove transitivity elimination, Hutchins proposes an equivalent
syntax-directed,
algorithmic
system with two different notions of reduction: an

equivalence reduction
that models
\beta
-reduction, and a
subtyping
reduction
that models small-step subtyping, where subtyping reduction subsumes
equivalence reduction. For short, we may write “reduction” for the
\beta
-reduction,
and “promotion” for the subtyping reduction. The declarative subtyping relation is shown
to be equivalent to the combination of subtyping and equivalence reduction sequences as
depicted below.

u\leq t\quad\textup{iff}\quad\textup{exists}~v~\textup{such that}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

In the algorithmic setting, transitivity can be shown to be admissible if these two
notions of reduction commute. Diagrammatically, commutativity ensures that a transitive
derivation which forms a “stair” of reduction and promotion steps can be flattened into
a single angle.

Despite his efforts, Hutchins failed to prove that commutativity holds in his algorithmic
system, which prevented him from proving transitivity elimination and type safety. The
culprit of this failure is exemplified by a very elementary case (depicted below), which
involves the reduction of a redex and the promotion of the formal parameter in the redex’s
abstraction to its annotation.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.t)\,v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)\,v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}

The redex
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)v
on the bottom-left corner of the diagram is reduced (in
the horizontal) to
v
, and promoted (in the vertical) to
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.t)v
. It is
then unclear how to complete the right edge of the diagram in a single step.

To tackle this problem, Hutchins resorted to simultaneous reduction and to the

decreasing diagrams
technique of
[
29
]
,
but he failed to assign depths to each reduction step so as to show
that reduction decreases in the way prescribed by the decreasing diagrams technique,
because
\beta
-reduction increases this depth.
Hutchins himself explains this failure in detail in his PhD thesis
(Section 2.7: Confluence and commutativity)
[
18
]
.

We reformulate the PSS theory by providing an alternative version of the algorithmic
system with a more fine-grained notion of subtyping, in which we can now prove the
commutativity result. Our version, which we dub
Machine-Based PSS
(MPSS for short),
uses a continuation stack mechanism, reminiscent of the Krivine Abstract Machine (KAM)

[
20
,
1
]
to track operands passed to abstractions. This mechanism enables a
direct proof of commutativity. Indeed, by explicitly managing operands on a stack, the
subtyping relation in MPSS exposes all the intermediate terms that are needed to complete
the commutativity diagram directly, without resorting to complex techniques like
decreasing diagrams. For instance, in our system, the subtyping relation

(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.t)v
doesn’t exist, instead the
variable
x
must first be promoted to the operand
v
, which later may be promoted to the
annotation
t
. Exposing this intermediate step, essentially disallowing
premature
promotion
that exists in Hutchins’ system, allows us to complete the above
commutativity diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.v)\,v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)\,v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

Our detailed contributions are the following:

•

We introduce MPSS, a reformulation of Hutchins’ PSS that, among other changes, keeps
track of the operands that have been passed at a particular scope by using a mechanism
reminiscent of the continuation stack of the KAM
[
20
,
1
]
.
MPSS is a more fine-grained system in the sense that the stack mechanism
exposes intermediate terms that were absent in Hutchins’ system.

•

We prove the commutativity of the subtyping and equivalence reductions of MPSS,
which enables transitivity elimination and the inversion lemma. Our proof of
commutativity is direct and proceeds by simple structural induction on terms.

•

We sketch how a type system can be derived from our alternative system,
thanks to transitivity being now admissible.

The remainder of this paper is organised as
follows. Section
2
introduces MPSS. Our system is a
reformulation of Hutchins’ algorithmic framework equipped with a stack mechanism inspired
by the Krivine Abstract Machine (KAM) to track operands, alongside two reductions for
subtyping and equivalence. Section
3
proves the commutativity of the
two reduction relations in MPSS. Section
4
sketches the design of a
type system built upon MPSS and discusses the path to proving its type safety.
Section
5
and
6
discuss related and future work, and
Section
7
concludes.

All the formal proofs of the lemmas and theorems stated in the paper are collected in the
appendix.

2
Machine-Based Pure Subtype Systems

Machine-Based PSS (MPSS for short) is a reformulation of PSS where the algorithmic
reductions are instrumented with a stack mechanism reminiscent of the Krivine Abstract
Machine (KAM)
[
20
,
1
]
.

MPSS is an extension of the pure lambda calculus with a constant symbol
\mathsf{Top}
for the most
general supertype and with subtype annotations in the formal parameters of abstractions.
Assuming a countably infinite set of variables
\mathrm{V}
ranged over by
x,y,\dots
, the
syntax of terms, contexts, and stacks is defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}t,u,v,\alpha,\ldots\in\Lambda&::=&x\mid\mathsf{Top}\mid(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)\mid(u\,v)\\
\Gamma&::=&\varepsilon\mid\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t\mid\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha\\
s&::=&\mathrm{nil}\mid\alpha::s\end{array}

A
variable

x\in\mathrm{V}
is a term. The term
\mathsf{Top}
is the
most general
supertype
, such that any other term is its subtype. An
abstraction

(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)
has a
parameter

x
, a
subtype annotation

t
, and a

body

u
. An
application

(u\,v)
has an
operator

u
and an

operand

v
. The Greek letter
\alpha
is a metavariable for terms that originate
as operands from the stack.

We assume the usual notions of
free
and
bound variables
, and the usual

capture-avoiding substitution
function, denoted by
u[x\backslash v]
, that replaces
the free occurrences of variable
x
in
u
by
v
, while avoiding the capture of any
bound variable in
u
. We consider terms that result after the renaming of bound variables
to be identical. When needed, we assume that
\alpha
-equivalence is applied at will to
avoid clashing of free variables.

An application of the form
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)v
is a
redex
. Contraction of a
redex is modelled by
\beta
-reduction, which rewrites
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)v
into

u[x\backslash v]
. Consider the following example of a redex involving the term

\lambda x\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.x
for
universal identity
. Since universal identity is a subtype
of
\mathsf{Top}
, the self-application of universal identity, i.e. the redex

(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.x)(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.x)
, reduces to

x[x\backslash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.x]
, which is equal to universal identity by the definition
of the substitution function. We assume conventional parenthesis-dropping conventions
where abstractions associate to the right and applications to the left, and applications
bind stronger than abstractions. We define normal forms as terms that are either Top,
functions that have a subtype annotation and a body in normal form, or a variable applied
to any number of normal forms.

Prevalidity of logical contexts

\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}

\frac{}{\varepsilon~\mathrm{prevalid}}\quad\textsc{right=Pv-Emp}\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}x\not\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)\mathrm{fv}(t)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)}{\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t~\mathrm{prevalid}}\quad\textsc{right=Pv-Ctx}\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}x\not\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)\mathrm{fv}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)}{\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha~\mathrm{prevalid}}\quad\textsc{right=Pv-EqA}

Prevalidity of extended contexts

\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}

\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}~\mathrm{prevalid}}\quad\textsc{right=Pv-Nil}\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}\mathrm{fv}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)}{\Gamma;\alpha::s~\mathrm{prevalid}}\quad\textsc{right=Pv-Sta}

Figure 1
:
Prevalidity in MPSS

Our system uses
logical contexts
(
contexts
for short)
\Gamma
to store
annotations from abstractions encountered within a given scope. A context
\Gamma
is a
sequence of
subtype annotations

x\,{\leq}\,t
and
equivalence annotations

x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha
. We write
x\,{\vartriangleleft}\,t
to denote either kind of annotation,
and throughout this paper the metavariable
\,{\vartriangleleft}\,
designates either
\,{\leq}\,

or
\,{\equiv}\,
. We use
\varepsilon
for the
empty context
and we write

\Gamma,x\,{\vartriangleleft}\,t
for the operation that appends annotation

x\vartriangleleft t
to the subtype annotations of
\Gamma
. By abuse of notation, we
write
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
for the concatenation of contexts
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. We
say that
x

has subtype
t
in

\Gamma
(written
x\,{\leq}\,t\in\Gamma
) if

x\,{\leq}\,t
is the rightmost annotation for
x
in
\Gamma
. Respectively, we say that
x

is equivalent to
\alpha
in

\Gamma
(written
x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha\in\Gamma
)
under similar conditions.

Equivalence
reduction

\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v

\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha\in\Gamma\Gamma;s\vdash\alpha\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha^{\prime}}{\Gamma;s\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right={Me-Pro}}\par\frac{\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v^{\prime}}{\Gamma;s\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)\,v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}[x\backslash v^{\prime}]}\quad\textsc{right=Me-Bet}\par\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma;s\vdash\mathsf{Top}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\mathsf{Top}}\quad\textsc{right=Me-Top}\par\frac{\Gamma;v::s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v^{\prime}}{\Gamma;s\vdash u\,v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}\,v^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Me-App}\par\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma;s\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}x}\quad\textsc{right=Me-Var}\par\frac{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash t\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t^{\prime}\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t;\mathrm{nil}\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.u^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Me-Fun}\par\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma;s\vdash\mathsf{Top}\,u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\mathsf{Top}}\quad\textsc{right=Me-TAp}\par\frac{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash t\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t^{\prime}\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\alpha::s\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.u^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Me-FOp}

Subtyping reduction

\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v

\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}x\,{\leq}\,t\in\Gamma}{\Gamma;s\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-Pro}\par\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\mathsf{Top}}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-Top}\par\frac{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v}{\Gamma;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-Equ}\par\frac{\Gamma;v::s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}}{\Gamma;s\vdash u\,v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}\,v}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-App}\par\frac{\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t;\mathrm{nil}\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-Fun}\par\frac{\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha;s\vdash u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\alpha::s\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-FOp}

Figure 2
:
Equivalence and subtyping reduction in MPSS

In order to accommodate the stack mechanism, we introduce
extended contexts

\Gamma;s
, which are logical contexts
\Gamma
coupled with a
continuation stack

s
. Extended contexts adapt the algorithmic reduction relations to the stack mechanism of
MPSS.

Figure
1
introduces the
prevalidity
condition on contexts
that ensures each annotation in a context mentions a different variable, to avoid clashing
of variable names. We say that
x

is in the domain of

\Gamma
(written

x\in\mathrm{dom}(\Gamma)
) if and only if an annotation for variable
x
occurs in
\Gamma
, i.e.,
there exists a term
t
(or a term
\alpha
) such that
x\,{\leq}\,t\in\Gamma
(or

x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha\in\Gamma
). The empty context
\varepsilon
is prevalid
(Rule
Pv-Emp
in Figure
1
). Adding a subtype annotation

x\,{\leq}\,t
, or an equivalence annotation
x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha
to a prevalid context

\Gamma
yields a prevalid context if the free variables of
t
(respectively,
\alpha
)
are in the domain of
\Gamma
, and if
x
is not already in the domain of
\Gamma

(Rules
Pv-Ctx
and
Pv-EqA
).
Prevalidity for extended contexts

(also defined in Figure
1
) stipulates that a prevalid context
coupled with an empty stack is prevalid (Rule
Pv-Nil
), and that an extended
context
\Gamma;\alpha::s
is prevalid if the free variables of
\alpha
are in the domain
of
\Gamma
(Rule
Pv-Sta
). We now present our reduction relations.

Figure
2
presents our equivalence (
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
)
and subtyping (
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
) reductions. We do away with a declarative system like
Hutchins’ system, and our MPSS resembles his algorithmic system (p. 58 of

[
18
]
) where we adopt simultaneous reduction upfront in order to simplify
the commutativity proofs (resembling p. 74 of
[
18
]
), and where contexts
are coupled with a stack in order to accommodate the stack mechanism. The equivalence
reduction
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
models a reflexive, small-step, simultaneous

\beta
-reduction. Rule
Me-Bet
performs
\beta
-reduction, while
Rule
Me-Pro
replaces a variable by its equivalent operand from the context.
Rules
Me-Var
and
Me-Top
allow the reduction relation to be reflexive.
Rule
Me-TAp
is a technical device from Hutchins’ formulation in

[
18
]
, whose objective is to accommodate terms with shape
\mathsf{Top}\,u
, which
may pop up in reductions, and which cannot be
\beta
-reduced by Rule
Me-Bet
(see
p. 59 of
[
18
]
for a further discussion on this issue).
Me-App

propagates promotion through unapplied applications by pushing the operand onto the stack
and proceeding with the operator, mimicking KAM’s stack management. Rules
Me-Fun

and
Me-FOp
distinguish between unapplied and applied abstractions:

Me-Fun
propagates promotion by enlarging the context with the abstraction’s
annotation and proceeding with the body, and
Me-FOp
promotes the body of an
applied abstraction by taking the operand from the stack and enlarging the context with an
equivalence annotation.

The subtyping reduction
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
defines promotion in a stack-aware manner.
Rule
Ms-Pro
promotes a variable to its subtype annotation, and
Rule
Ms-Top
promotes any term to
\mathsf{Top}
. Rule
Ms-Equ
subsumes equivalence
reduction. Rules
Ms-App
,
Ms-Fun
and
Ms-FOp
are the
counterparts of the equivalence reduction rules
Me-App
,
Me-Fun
and

Me-FOp
respectively. Because Hutchins’ declarative system is not contravariant,
its algorithmic system, which we took inspiration from, does not change the type
annotation of abstractions: in his system, there is no rule

\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.u
with assumption

t^{\prime}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t
. The main motivation for this design choice is to avoid the
undecidability of the related typechecking, as described in
[
26
]
, and to avoid
complicating the meta-theory even more. Our system follows the same principle, both Rules

Ms-Fun
and
Ms-FOp
do not change the type annotation, and in general
there is no reduction rule to change the type annotations, except from equivalence
reductions that are allowed. Handling the interaction between the stack and the context is
the cornerstone to prove the commutativity result, and to avoid the premature promotion
issues that plagued Hutchins’ approach.

To illustrate our stack mechanism, consider the following subtyping reduction derivation
(prevalidity derivations are elided for brevity):

\frac{\frac{\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\Gamma;s~\mathrm{prevalid}}\frac{\frac{\ldots}{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}}\frac{\ldots}{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v}}{\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,v;\mathrm{nil}\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v}\hskip 7.3612pt\textsc{right=Me-Pro}}{\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,v;\mathrm{nil}\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{right=Ms-Equ}}{\Gamma;v::\mathrm{nil}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.v)}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-FOp}}{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x)v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.v)v}\quad\textsc{right=Ms-App}

Rule
Ms-App
pushes the operand
v
onto the stack. Inside the abstraction, the
variable
x
is not promoted to its type
t
but is instead reduced to the operand
v

retrieved from the context, which was placed there by
Ms-FOp
after popping from
the stack. Keeping track of operands prevents the premature promotion that caused
Hutchins’ commutativity proof to fail.

As in Hutchins’ PSS, we define a subtyping relation
\leq
, on which we wish to prove that
transitivity is admissible in the next section, as follows, with
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow\hskip-11.99998pt\rightarrow\hskip 1.99997pt}}}
being the
transitive closure of
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
:

u\leq t\quad\textup{iff}\quad\textup{exists}~v~\textup{such that}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

3
Transitivity elimination

As explained in the introduction, proving transitivity elimination requires us to show
that the equivalence (
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
) and subtyping (
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
) reductions commute,
which we do below in this section. In order to state the commutativity result, we first
define a reduction relation on extended contexts, which captures the evolution of
annotations during reduction.

Reduction of extended context

\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}

\frac{\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash t\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t^{\prime}}{\Gamma,x\,{\vartriangleleft}\,t;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime},x\,{\vartriangleleft}\,t^{\prime};s^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Ct-Ann}\par\frac{\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\alpha\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha^{\prime}}{\Gamma;\alpha::s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};\alpha^{\prime}::s^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{right=Ct-Stk}

With this definition, we can now state our main commutativity result.

Lemma 1

(
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
and
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
strongly commute)
.

Let
\Gamma;s
be an extended context. Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, and
t_{2}
be terms. If

\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
, then
for any extended context
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
t_{3}
, such that
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
and

\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.

Diagrammatically, the existence of the solid arrows implies the existence of the dashed
arrows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

The generalisation over all possible resulting contexts
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}
is crucial for the
inductive proof. Indeed, consider the following commutation diagram, with context

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

The only possible arrow that could exist, apart from a promotion of the whole term to

\mathsf{Top}
, is a promotion of the variable
x
to its subtype annotation
t^{\prime}
. Therefore, the
top right term is
\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.t^{\prime}
, and the diagram is completed as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.t^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

However, in the proof of this theorem, everything works by induction on the terms.
Therefore, starting from the original diagram, we apply the induction hypothesis on the
following diagram, with context
\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t;\mathrm{nil}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces?}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

And as we have seen above, we must complete this diagram with
t^{\prime}
being the top right
term, as the only possible completion of the original diagram is the term

\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime}.t^{\prime}
. Therefore, the completed diagram by the induction hypothesis
must be:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

Hence the requirement that the context of the right arrow must be

\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t^{\prime};\mathrm{nil}
, and therefore the formulation of the theorem with a
generalisation over contexts.

Similarly, our equivalence reduction relation satisfies the diamond property, on which the
result above rests. A similar generalisation over contexts is required, with two different
reduced extended contexts however, as both the top and the right edge can now do variable
promotions thanks to Rule
Me-Pro
. The pathological case is the following, with
context
\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.\alpha_{0}\,x)\,\alpha_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2})\,\alpha_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x\,x)\,\alpha_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.x\,\alpha_{0})\,\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

To solve this pathological case in the induction, one of the sub-induction calls is the
following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}\,x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}\,\alpha_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\,x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x\,\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

And similarly as above, the top edge is completed with extended context

\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{2};\mathrm{nil}
, whereas the right edge is completed with extended
context
\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{1};\mathrm{nil}
. Both these contexts satisfy the requirement
of the theorem (that is, we have

\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{2};\mathrm{nil}
and

\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{1};\mathrm{nil}
).

Lemma 2

(
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
has the diamond property)
.

Let
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}
be an extended context. Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, and
t_{2}
be terms. If

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
, then for any extended contexts

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
, there exists a term
t_{3}
such that

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
and

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.

Moreover, for any variable
x
, if in the derivation of

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
(respectively

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
) there isn’t an application of the Rule

Me-Pro
that makes a promotion of variable
x
, then in the derivation

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
(respectively

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
) there won’t be an application of the Rule

Me-Pro
that makes a promotion of variable
x
.

Diagrammatically:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

With commutativity and the diamond property established, we can prove that our system
enjoys the transitivity elimination property.

Theorem 3

(Transitivity is admissible)
.

Let
\Gamma;s
be an extended context. Let
u
and
v
be terms. If

\Gamma;s\vdash u\leq^{*}v
then
\Gamma;s\vdash u\leq v
.

In order to illustrate our result, and highlight the differences between PSS and MPSS, we
detail below an example of an application of
Theorem
3
:

Example of transitivity elimination

Consider the Church encodings for type ‘Unit’ (the type with only one inhabitant, term

\mathit{Unit}
below), the encoding of the constructor ‘single’ of type Unit (term
\mathit{single}

below), and an arbitrary well-formed subtype
A
that encodes some interesting type. Let
the identity over the elements of subtype
A
be the term
\mathit{id}_{A}
below, and consider an
arbitrary well-formed term
a
of subtype
A
such that some derivation

\varepsilon;\mathrm{nil}\vdash a\leq^{*}A
exists which may possibly be as lengthy or
complicated as it may be.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{Unit}&=&\lambda y\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.\lambda x\,{\leq}\,y.y\\
\mathit{single}&=&\lambda y\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.\lambda x\,{\leq}\,y.x\\
A&=&\langle\text{arbitrary subtype}\rangle\\
\mathit{id}_{A}&=&\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x\\
a&=&\langle\text{arbitrary subtype of $A$}\rangle\end{array}

Consider the following derivation:

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{id}_{A}\,a\leq\mathit{single}\,A\,a\leq^{*}\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a

Figure
3
details all the elementary steps
(diagrammatically) of the above algorithmic derivation. We illustrate the proof of
Theorem
3
over this diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.A)\,a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.a)\,a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x)\,a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda y\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{Top}.\lambda x\,{\leq}\,y.x)\,A\,a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x)\,a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a\leq^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

Figure 3
:
Derivation of
\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x)\,a\leq\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a

Theorem
3
ensures that the transitivity step
collecting the two derivations
\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{id}_{A}\,a\leq\mathit{single}\,A\,a
and

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{single}\,A\,a\leq^{*}\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a
can be removed, obtaining a
transitivity-free derivation

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{id}_{A}\,a\leq\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a

Our constructive proof produces a transitivity-free derivation of

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{single}\,A\,a\leq^{*}\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a
(i.e., flattens it into

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash\mathit{single}\,A\,a\leq\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a
witnessed by the straight vertical
and horizontal paths in the middle of the diagram), and then applies the commutativity
theorem to complete the upper-right corner of the diagram, obtaining the transitivity-free
derivation
\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x)\,a\leq\mathit{Unit}\,A\,a
. This proof is possible
in our algorithmic system thanks to the operand stack and Rule
Ms-FOp
which
exposes the term
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.a)\,a
, and to the fact that the derivation

\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.a)\,a\leq^{*}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.A)\,a
in the
middle of the diagram preserves the possibly convoluted derivation

\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,a;\mathrm{nil}\vdash a\leq^{*}A
as a sub-derivation (marked with

\ddots
). Although preserving this convoluted derivation seems at first glance
counter-intuitive, it gives the opportunity to apply the induction hypothesis of the
Theorem
3
to the term

(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.a)\,a
, which will flatten the convoluted derivation. Contrary to
ours, Hutchins’ attempts at proving that transitivity is admissible reduce the complexity
of the intermediate step by avoiding the convoluted derivation upfront (by directly taking
the reduction
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.x)\,a\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,A.A)\,a
which
promotes the formal parameter
a
of the redex to its annotation
A
).

But this premature promotion of a formal parameter to its type annotation is in fact
counter-productive, which manifests in the failure of Hutchins’ proof of the commutativity
theorem, where the premature promotion prevents one from completing the diagram for
redexes with a transitivity-free right edge.

This result was the missing cornerstone in Hutchins’ original theory, and its proof here
is necessary for establishing type safety. In the next section, we present what can be a
type-safe theory based on our MPSS.

4
Towards a Type-Safe System

Thanks to the above commutative system, we can now sketch the design of a type-safe
system. The static semantics for this system is defined by a
well-formedness

judgment, presented in Figure
4
, which is the static
condition for type safety. Rules
Wf-PrS
and
Wf-PrE
ensure that a
variable is well-formed if and only if it occurs in a prevalid context (respectively, in a
subtype or an equivalence annotation). Rule
Wf-Top
ensures that
\mathsf{Top}
is
universally well-formed. Rule
Wf-Fun
ensures that an abstraction is well-formed
if and only if it has a well-formed annotation, and its body is well-formed in a context
enlarged with the abstraction annotation. Finally, Rule
Wf-App
ensures that an
application is well-formed if and only if it has an operator that is a well-subtype of a
function, and an operand that is a well-subtype of the operator’s subtype annotation.

Well-formedness depends on the
well-subtyping
relation
\leq_{\mathrm{wf}}
, which in turn
subsumes
well-equivalence

\equiv_{\mathrm{wf}}
. The rules for well-subtyping connect the
static system to our dynamic reduction semantics. For example, Rule
Ws-Lf2
states
that
v
is a well-subtype of
t
if
v
promotes to some
v^{\prime}
that is itself a
well-subtype of
t
.

Term well-formedness

\Gamma\vdash t~\mathrm{wf}

\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}x\,{\leq}\,t\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash x~\mathrm{wf}}\quad\textsc{right=Wf-PrS}\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\vdash x~\mathrm{wf}}\quad\textsc{right=Wf-PrE}\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{Top}~\mathrm{wf}}\quad\textsc{right=Wf-Top}\frac{\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t\vdash u~\mathrm{wf}\Gamma\vdash t~\mathrm{wf}}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u~\mathrm{wf}}\quad\textsc{right=Wf-Fun}\frac{\Gamma\vdash u\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.\mathsf{Top}\Gamma\vdash v\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}t}{\Gamma\vdash u\,v~\mathrm{wf}}\quad\textsc{right=Wf-App}

Transitive well-subtyping

\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}^{*}t

\frac{\Gamma\vdash v~\mathrm{wf}\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t\Gamma\vdash t~\mathrm{wf}}{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}^{*}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Sub}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}^{*}u\Gamma\vdash u~\mathrm{wf}\Gamma\vdash u\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}^{*}t}{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}^{*}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Trs}

Well-subtyping

\Gamma\vdash u\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t

\frac{\Gamma~\mathrm{prevalid}}{\Gamma\vdash t\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Rfl}\par\frac{\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v^{\prime}\Gamma\vdash v^{\prime}\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t}{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Lf1}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash v~\mathrm{wf}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v^{\prime}\Gamma\vdash v^{\prime}~\mathrm{wf}\Gamma\vdash v^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{wf}}t}{\Gamma\vdash v\leq_{\mathrm{wf}}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Lf2}\par\frac{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t^{\prime}\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash t\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vdash v\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}t}\quad\textsc{right=Ws-Rgh}

Figure 4
:
Well-subtyping in MPSS

We define type safety as
progress
(well-formed terms either are in normal form or
can be reduced), and
preservation
(subtyping relations between terms are preserved
under reduction by the operational semantics below). We define the operational semantics
as a context-based reduction semantics
[
13
]
: the evaluation
\mapsto
is defined
as the congruence closure (Rule
Os-Con
and evaluation contexts
\mathsf{C}
) of

\beta
-reduction (Rule
Os-Bet
).

Operational Semantics

t\mapsto t^{\prime}

\frac{t\mapsto t^{\prime}}{\mathsf{C}[t]\mapsto\mathsf{C}[t^{\prime}]}\quad\textsc{right=Os-Con}\par\frac{}{(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)v\mapsto u[x\backslash v]}\quad\textsc{right=Os-Bet}\par\mathsf{C}::=\square\mid(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,\mathsf{C}.t)\mid(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.\mathsf{C})\mid(\mathsf{C}\,t)\mid(t\,\mathsf{C})

Well-formedness depends on the
well-subtyping
relation defined in
Figure
4
. Well-subtyping (
\leq_{\mathrm{wf}}
) subsumes

well-equivalence
(
\equiv_{\mathrm{wf}}
). We use the meta-variable
\,{\vartriangleleft}\,
and
define (
\,{\vartriangleleft}\,_{\mathrm{wf}}
), which captures both relations, and similarly for their
transitive closure (
\,{\vartriangleleft}\,^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}
). By a slight abuse of language, and to
simplify the text, we write “well-subtyping” to refer to the meta-notation
(
\vartriangleleft_{\mathrm{wf}}
) in the paragraph that follows.

Figure
4
introduces
transitive
well-subtyping
, the straightforward transitive closure of the well-subtyping relation
(Rules
Ws-Sub
and
Ws-Trs
). Next in the figure is
well-subtyping
,
which is defined similarly to the diagrammatic subtyping relation
\leq
defined in
Section
2
, but with extra requirements on the well-formedness
of the terms involved. Rule
Ws-Rfl
states that well-subtyping is reflexive.
Rule
Ws-Lf1
ensures that
v
is a well-subtype of
t
if and only if
v^{\prime}
is a
well-subtype of
t
and
v
reduces to
v^{\prime}
. Rule
Ws-Lf2
ensures that
v
is a
well-subtype of
t
if and only if
v^{\prime}
is a well-subtype of
t
and
v
promotes to
v^{\prime}
,
but with the extra requirements that both
v
and
v^{\prime}
are well-formed, whose role is
explained in a minute. Rule
Ws-Rgh
ensures that
v
is a well-subtype of
t
if
and only if
v
is a well-subtype of
t^{\prime}
and
t
reduces to
t^{\prime}
.

Thanks to these definitions, we may define type safety in our calculus as the following
progress and preservation theorems.

Theorem 4

(Progress)
.

Let
t
be a term. For every logical context
\Gamma
, if
\Gamma\vdash t~\mathrm{wf}
then
either
t
is in normal form, or there exists a term
t^{\prime}
such that
t\mapsto t^{\prime}
.

Theorem 5

(Preservation)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a logical context. Let
t
,
t^{\prime}
, and
u
be terms. If

\Gamma\vdash t\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}u
and
t\mapsto t^{\prime}
, then

\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}u
.

The proof of type preservation (Theorem
5
) rests on showing that
reduction preserves term well-formedness. Lemma
6
establishes
this fact.

Lemma 6

(Evaluation preserves well-formedness)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a logical context. Let
t
and
t^{\prime}
be terms such that
t\mapsto t^{\prime}

and such that
\Gamma\vdash t~\mathrm{wf}
. We have
\Gamma\vdash t^{\prime}~\mathrm{wf}
.

A crucial case in the proof of Lemma
6
is
\beta
-reduction,
where a well-formed application
(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u)\,\alpha
reduces to

u[x\backslash\alpha]
. To handle this case, we need to show that well-formedness is
preserved by substitution. The following lemma provides this guarantee, where the premise

\Gamma\vdash\alpha\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}t
comes from the well-formedness of the original
term.

Lemma 7

(Substitution preserves well-formedness)
.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be logical contexts, and let
t
,
u
and
\alpha
be terms.
Let
x
be a variable. If
\Gamma,x\,{\leq}\,t,\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash u~\mathrm{wf}
, and

\Gamma\vdash\alpha\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}t
, then

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}[x\backslash\alpha]\vdash u[x\backslash\alpha]~\mathrm{wf}
.

Our subtyping relation in MPSS is context-dependent, unlike in PSS where it isn’t, which
implies that Lemma
7
relies on the conjecture collected
below, which states that the well-subtyping relation is independent of the surrounding
program structure, on certain contexts named
covariant contexts
defined as follows:

\mathsf{Co}::=\square\mid(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.\mathsf{Co})\mid(\mathsf{Co}\,t)

Conjecture 8

(Well-subtyping is context independent)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a logical context and
u
and
t
be terms such that

\Gamma\vdash u\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}t
. Let
\mathsf{Co}
be a covariant context such that both

\mathsf{Co}[u]
and
\mathsf{Co}[t]
are well-formed in
\Gamma
. We conjecture that

\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{Co}[u]\leq^{*}_{\mathrm{wf}}\mathsf{Co}[t]
.

We believe that the conjecture holds with the additional well-formedness requirements that
both
v
and
v^{\prime}
are well-formed in Rule
Ws-Lf2
above.

All in all, type safety, which is the combination of progress (Theorem
4
)
and preservation (Theorem
5
), holds under the assumption that
Conjecture
8
holds. The proof that both these theorems hold
under the assumption that Conjecture
8
holds can be found in
the appendix.

Establishing this conjecture is the final step towards a complete proof of type safety.
The sketch of a complete type system provided in this section demonstrates a path forward,
built upon the solid foundation of our commutativity result. Future work could either
adopt this sketch and complete the proof of type safety, or develop a different type
system that leverages the commutativity result established in
Section
3
to establish type safety in a different way.

5
Related work

PSS naturally subsumes dependent types and higher-order subtyping, so we comment on both
ideas.

There are many works related to dependent types. For instance in Cayenne

[
4
]
, Idris
[
7
]
or Haskell

[
21
]
, dependent types are an extension of the language, in contrast to
our case where dependent types are built-in as there is no distinction between terms and
types.

Other languages mainly use dependent types for theorem proving, for instance Coq

[
12
]
or Agda
[
6
]
. Our language is not strongly normalising,
and our main goal is not theorem proving as we cannot use the language as a proof system,
since applying the Curry-Howard correspondence to PSS in a consistent way is still an open
question.

To accommodate the Curry-Howard correspondence, one possible approach is to distinguish
between potentially non-terminating terms and terminating terms, either with monads or via
other means
[
23
,
24
]
.
\lambda^{\theta}
is a
calculus that explores this idea

[
10
,
9
,
28
]
. It
combines proofs and programs into a single language that distinguishes two fragments, a
logical fragment where every term is strongly normalising that is suited to write proofs,
and a programmatic fragment that is Turing complete and lacks termination. Such systems
allow so-called
freedom of speech
, which is the ability to write (terminating)
proofs about non-terminating terms.

Subtyping extensions of type systems have been considered. In 1997, Chen proposed

\lambda_{C_{\leq}}

[
11
]
, an extension of the calculus of constructions

\lambda_{C}
with subtyping. However, Chen’s
\lambda_{C_{\leq}}
supports neither top types
nor bounded quantification as we do in PSS.

Various extensions of System F, in order to equip it with subtyping, have also been
considered in the literature. Within these we find System F
≤

[
8
]
and System F
{}_{\leq}^{\omega}

[
25
]
, which
respectively add subtyping and higher-order subtyping capabilities. PSS subsumes the
version of System F
≤
without contravariant arrow-types
[
18
]
.

The origin of mixing terms and types goes back to the introduction of pure type systems

[
22
]
, which generalises the seminal works on lambda calculi with types
by
[
5
]
. These systems have the ability to unify types and terms under the same
kind, but there is a typing relation instead of a subtyping one. Moreover, in some of
these pure type systems, the type
\bot
is inhabited. This gives rise to the well-known
Girard’s paradox (see
[
17
]
). In our system, there is not a
primitive
\bot
subtype, but we could encode such a subtype to be inhabited by itself,
and that would be unproblematic since our aim is not to use PSS as a theorem prover.

Systems where terms can be used in certain ways as types have been considered. Aspinall
studied the combination of subtyping with singleton types

[
3
]
. Singleton types are close to what we study, as in our
calculus, terms can be seen as singleton types of this unique value. We have for instance,
for every term
t
,
\varepsilon\vdash t\leq t
, which would be translated to

\vdash t:\{t\}
in a system with singleton types.

Other attempts to unify typing and subtyping exist. A more conservative attempt that tries
to unify typing and subtyping is
[
31
]
. This work has two major
differences with our approach. First, in
[
31
]
the subtyping relation
still tracks types (their unification of subtyping and typing is more conservative than
ours). Second, in PSS functions and function (sub)types are defined with the same syntax,
and therefore they are both instances of the same construct, which is not the case in

[
31
]
.

Because many interesting features described in
[
18
]
are related to
modules, other type systems especially related to objects have been studied. Our work
shares foundational goals with the Dependent Object Types (DOT) calculus, the theoretical
basis for Scala 3
[
2
,
27
]
. Both PSS and DOT aim to create
powerful, unified type systems that support advanced modularity, and provide a formal
basis for solving challenges akin to the expression problem, the tag-elimination problem,
and others. DOT achieves this, among other features, through path-dependent types, where
the type of a member,
p.T
, depends on the term
p
. Because DOT allows objects to
contain types as members, it provides a form of dependent typing. PSS takes a more radical
approach by collapsing types and terms into a single syntactic category, where the
traditional typing relation (
:
) is completely replaced by a universal subtyping relation
(
\leq
). This design is what enables PSS’s key features described in Sections 3 and 4 of

[
18
]
.

6
Future work

As described in Section
4
, it is left as future work to define a type
system that is built upon MPSS and to prove its type safety. Another interesting area to
explore is the link between our system and Hutchins’ system, to determine if both
well-formed notions are equivalent.

Once this system is defined, it is also left as future work to obtain an algorithm that
typechecks terms in MPSS. Hutchins describes an algorithm for his system in

[
18
]
, but one should adapt his algorithm to this new system, and study
upon which conditions it terminates. One possible approach is to use the operational
relevance of terms, for which there exist some arguments that lead them to terminate. We
leave as future work to explore other practical algorithms which terminate on
operationally relevant terms.

Another venue of future work is to study whether PSS and MPSS are Turing-complete. To the
best of our knowledge, whether PSS is Turing-complete has not been formally proven yet.
The argument in favor of Turing-completeness rests on the existence of a (well-formed)
looping combinator in PSS, which would consist of a family of terms
L_{n}
such that

L_{n}\,f

\beta
-reduces to
f(L_{n+1}\,f)
for any
f
. Such a looping combinator
exists for System
\lambda*

[
16
]
, which can be encoded into Hutchins’ PSS
thanks to the encoding of System
\lambda*
in Hutchins’ PSS, provided on page 47 of

[
18
]
. In order to establish Turing-completeness of PSS, one should now
adapt the argument made by
[
14
]
in Section 2 or the argument made in

[
15
]
. There is, however, no simple known expression of a looping combinator in
System
\lambda*
. The looping combinator from
[
16
]
can only be derived
mechanically, and working with it directly is proven to be hard as its length is more than
40 pages (see the discussion on the logical consistency and impredicativity of
System
\lambda_{\vartriangleleft}
on pages 48–51 of
[
18
]
). It may therefore be
difficult to conclude about Turing-completeness of either PSS or MPSS, which is left as
future work.

7
Conclusion

This paper presents a variant of the PSS originally introduced by Hutchins in

[
18
]
, which we name MPSS, and proves that our reformulation enjoys a key
commutation property which was missing in Hutchins’ system. We also sketch how a type
system can be derived from MPSS, which could lead to a type-safe system. To the best of
our knowledge PSS and MPSS are the first theories that unify harmoniously terms and types
by using a single kind where everything is a subtype.

Meta-theories of languages with advanced features on types are notoriously difficult to
work with. We believe our work could help in laying the foundations for a new approach
that mixes terms and types in innovative ways.

Appendix A
Theorems of the text

Theorem
1
[
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
and
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}

strongly commute]

Let
\Gamma;s
be an extended context.
Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
be terms.
If
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}

then for all extended context
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}
with
\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}
,
there exists a term
t_{3}
,
such that
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
and
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.

Diagrammatically, where the solid arrows are the conditions for the existence of the dashed arrows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

Proof.

By induction on the term structure of
t_{0}
.

We consider cases according to the last
two rules that are applied respectively in the derivation of the horizontal and
vertical edges of the diagram above, starting by the bottom-left corner.

The cases where bottom rule is rule
Me-Top
or respectively rule
Me-TAp
,
then the missing top edge is by rule
Me-Top
or respectively
Me-TAp
,
and the missing right edge is by rule
Ms-Top
.

If the vertical edge rule is
Ms-Top
,
then the missing top edge is by rule
Me-Top
,
and the missing right edge is by rule
Ms-Top
.

If the vertical edge is
Ms-Equ
, then we have

\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
.
By reflexivity of
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
(Proposition
18
), we have

\Gamma;s\rightarrowtail\Gamma;s
.
Hence by Lemma
2
we have

\Gamma;s\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
and
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.
Thanks to rule
Ms-Equ
, from the latter derivation, we obtain
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.

We know that if the rule
Me-Pro
appears in the derivation tree
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
,
then we have in fact
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
, and as a result the results follows from
Lemma
2
as outlined by the paragraph outlined above.
We therefore assume that the rule
Me-Pro
doesn’t appear in the derivation tree
\Gamma;s\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
,
an information useful only for the last case of this lemma.

Else, for the general case we distinguish the sub-cases below:

Case
Me-Pro
with
Ms-Pro
:

We need to prove the diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

•

with

•

By prevalidity of the context, we cannot have two distinct annotation for

in the context,
and therefore this case is impossible: there cannot be a subtype annotation and an equivalence
annotation in the prevalid context
\Gamma
.

As explained in the introduction paragraph of this lemma,
we know that in the derivation tree of

then the rule

isn’t used
(which is because otherwise we’d have
\Gamma,x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}

and we conclude by Lemma
2
).
Therefore this derivation is in fact
\Gamma;s\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}
.

By induction hypothesis on
u_{0}
, there exists a term
u_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma;s\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

•

\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

It is important to note that
x
is a fresh variable created by the lambdas of this case,
and therefore by alpha conversion there is no instance of
x
in
s
, hence
s[x\backslash v_{1}]=s
.
By a similar reasoning, we know that there is no
x
isn’t in the domain of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
As a result, the derivation
\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
do not make a promotion of
x
.
Hence by Lemma
30
, we obtain

\Gamma^{\prime};s^{\prime}\vdash u_{1}[x\backslash v_{1}]\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\leq}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}[x\backslash v_{1}]
.

By rule
Me-Bet
, we obtain

\Gamma;s\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u_{2})\,v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}[x\backslash v_{1}]
from

\Gamma;s\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
and
\Gamma;\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}
.

The filled diagram is therefore the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u_{2})\,v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{3}[x\backslash v_{1}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t.u_{0})\,v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}[x\backslash v_{1}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

∎

Lemma
2
(
\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}
has the diamond property).

Let
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}
be an extended context.
Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, and
t_{2}
be terms.
If
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
,
then for any extended contexts

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
such that
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
,
there exists a term
t_{3}

such that
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
.

Moreover, for any variable
x
, if in the derivation of
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}

(respectively
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}
)
there isn’t an application of the Rule
Me-Pro
that makes a promotion of variable
x
,
then in the derivation
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash t_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}

(respectively
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash t_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{3}
)
there won’t be an application of the Rule
Me-Pro
that makes a promotion of variable
x
.

Diagrammatically, the existence of the solid arrows implies the existence of the dashed arrows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

Proof.

We now proceed by induction on the derivation tree of
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
.
We consider cases according to the last
two rules that are applied respectively in the derivation of the horizontal and
vertical edges of the diagram above, starting by the bottom-left corner. The base case
corresponds to when these two rules are both
Me-Top
(respectively both
Me-Var
),
for which the lemma holds by using the rule
Me-Top
(respectively
Me-Var
) for the missing edges.
For the general case we distinguish the subcases below:

Case
Me-Pro
and
Me-Var
:

We need to prove the diagram below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

By assumption, we have the following reductions:

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}x
.

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
with
x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
.

Let
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
be extended contexts such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
.

By Lemma
36
,

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
implies

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}
.
Because
\Gamma_{0}
is of the form
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime},x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{\prime\prime}
,

we have
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{2},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.

By multiple use of the rule
Ct-Ann
, we have
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime};\mathrm{nil}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{2}
.

By weakening (Lemma
19
), we have
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{2}
.

By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
, there exists a term
\alpha_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash\alpha_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash\alpha_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}

By rule
Me-Pro
, we have
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash x\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}
from

x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{1}\in\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash\alpha_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}
.

The filled diagram is therefore the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

Case
Me-Pro
and
Me-Pro
:

We need to prove the diagram below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

By assumption, we have the following reductions:

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
with
x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
.

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{2}
with
x\,{\equiv}\,\alpha_{0}\in\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{2}
.

Let
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
be extended contexts such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
.

By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash\alpha_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{1}
, there exists a term

such that:

•

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash\alpha_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash\alpha_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\alpha_{3}

The filled diagram is therefore the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

Case
Me-App
and
Me-App
:

We need to prove the diagram below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}\,v_{2}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}\,v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}\,v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

By assumption, we have the following reductions:

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\,v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{1}\,v_{1}
by rule
Me-App
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{1}

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\,v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}\,v_{2}
by rule
Me-App
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{2}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}

Let
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
be extended contexts such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
.

By rule
Ct-Stk
, we know:

•

\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};v_{1}::s_{1}
from
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};v_{2}::s_{2}
from
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{2}

By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{1}
, there exists a term
u_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1};v_{1}::s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};v_{2}::s_{2}\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

By Lemma
36
, respectively
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}

implies respectively

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}
and
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}
.
By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}
, there exists a term
v_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

We now have:

•

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\,v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}\,v_{3}
by rule
Me-App

from
\Gamma_{1};v_{1}::s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
and
\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash u_{2}\,v_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}\,v_{3}
by rule
Me-App

from
\Gamma_{2};v_{2}::s_{2}\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
and
\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

The filled diagram is therefore the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}\,v_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{3}\,v_{3}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}\,v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

Case
Me-App
with
Me-Bet
:

We need to prove the diagram below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}[x\backslash v_{2}]}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0})v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1})v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

By assumption, we have the following reductions:

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0})v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1})v_{1}
by rule
Me-App
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};v_{0}::s_{0}\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1}
.
By rule
Me-FOp
, this implies
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{1}

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0})v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}[x\backslash v_{2}]
by rule
Me-Bet
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{2}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}

Let
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
be extended contexts such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
.

By weakening (Lemma
19
), we obtain
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}

from
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}
,
and we also know that the derivation
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}
do not make a promotion of
x
to
v_{0}
.

We have by rule
Ct-Ann
,

\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{1};s_{1}
from
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}
.
Similarly we have
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{2};s_{2}
.
By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}
, there exists a term
u_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{1};s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{2};s_{2}\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

Because the derivation
\Gamma_{0},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{0};s_{0}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}
do not make any promotion of
x
to
v_{0}
,
then the induction process ensures that the derivation
\Gamma_{1},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{1};s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}

do not make any promotion of
x
to
v_{1}
.
As a result we have
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
.

By Lemma
36
, respectively
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}

implies respectively

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}
and
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}
.
By induction hypothesis on
\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{1}
, there exists a term
v_{3}
such that:

•

\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

We now have:

•

\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1})v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}[x\backslash v_{3}]
by rule
Me-Bet

from
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}\vdash u_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
and
\Gamma_{1};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{1}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

•

\Gamma_{2};s_{2}\vdash u_{2}[x\backslash v_{2}]\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}[x\backslash v_{3}]
by Lemma
32

from
\Gamma_{2},x\,{\equiv}\,v_{2};s_{2}\vdash u_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{3}
and
\Gamma_{2};\mathrm{nil}\vdash v_{2}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}v_{3}

The filled diagram is therefore the following:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}[x\backslash v_{2}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{3}[x\backslash v_{3}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0})v_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1})v_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

Case

with

:

We need to prove the diagram below:

.

.

Case

with

:

We need to prove the diagram below:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{2}.u_{2}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\equiv}

By assumption, we have the following reductions:

•

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{1}.u_{1}
by rule
Me-Fun
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{1}

–

\Gamma_{0},x\,{\leq}\,t_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{1}

•

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{0}.u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}\lambda x\,{\leq}\,t_{2}.u_{2}
by rule
Me-Fun
, which gives us:

–

\Gamma_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash t_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}t_{2}

–

\Gamma_{0},x\,{\leq}\,t_{0};\mathrm{nil}\vdash u_{0}\mathop{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\equiv}}{{\longrightarrow}}}u_{2}

Let
\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
be extended contexts such that

\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{1};s_{1}
and
\Gamma_{0};s_{0}\rightarrowtail\Gamma_{2};s_{2}
.
Because by assumption of this case we have
